21:[["$","p",null,{"children":["$","span",null,{"className":"katex","role":"$undefined","aria-label":"$undefined","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"<span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"></span>"}}]}],"\n",["$","section",null,{"className":"my-6 rounded border border-zinc-200 bg-white/60 p-4","data-component":"learning-outcomes","children":[["$","header",null,{"className":"font-sans text-xs uppercase tracking-[0.15em] text-zinc-500","children":"What you’ll learn"}],["$","div",null,{"className":"mt-2 font-serif text-[1rem] leading-relaxed text-zinc-800","children":[["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"(What.)"}]," By the end of the day, students can explain ",["$","em",null,{"children":"embedded agency"}],": why an agent built into the world it acts on (made of the same stuff, smaller than its environment, with no clean input/output boundary, able to model and modify itself) breaks the standard dualistic picture. 2a:["$","h3",null,{"id":"22-main-content","children":"2.2 Main content"}]
2b:["$","p",null,{"children":["Agent foundations is not one theory but several research strands (research directions), each attacking a different facet of the same problem: how to reason about, and build, ",["$","em",null,{"children":"stable"}]," agents that are ",["$","em",null,{"children":"embedded"}]," in the world they act on. This day is organized around five such strands (consequentialist foundations; Löb's theorem and tiling agents; logical induction; optimization and thermodynamics; descriptive agent foundations). Each student pre-reads one strand in depth before the day; the morning lecture supplies the spine that connects them; the shared fundamental readings and a small-group discussion knit them into a single picture; and an exercise session makes a few of the results rigorous."]}]
2c:["$","p",null,{"children":"Read top to bottom, this section runs: the unifying threads (the morning lecture), then the fundamental readings that give a bird's-eye view, then the five strands each with its readings, then the cross-pollination discussion, the exercises, and a self-check."}]
2d:["$","h4",null,{"id":"221-morning-lecture","children":"2.2.1 Morning lecture"}]
2e:["$","ul",null,{"children":["\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Why agent foundations."}]," We may need key safety properties to hold on the first critical try, for an agent far more capable than any we can study directly. Agent foundations looks for the properties capable agents share ",["$","em",null,{"children":"in general"}],", so we can reason about them in advance."]}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Reflective stability."}]," A safety property only matters if it survives the agent's own self-modification: a capable agent may rewrite its code, build a more capable successor, or revise its world model. A property is ",["$","em",null,{"children":"reflectively stable"}]," if it is invariant under all of these. Working assumption: under enough optimization pressure, a property persists only if there is some reason it must. Reflective stability is the thread running through every strand."]}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Robust concepts (\"true names\")."}]," Goodhart's law says a proxy that becomes a target stops measuring what we want. So a theory of agency must be built from concepts that do not break under optimization, the \"true names\" of optimization, goals, world models, and embeddedness."]}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Two pathways of impact (following C. Wyeth)."}]," ",["$","em",null,{"children":"Modelling"}],": build an abstract mathematical model of an idealised capable agent and use it to show why a given alignment proposal would fail. ",["$","em",null,{"children":"Implementation"}],": develop a theory far enough to build and inspect an actual system, often favouring a modular architecture (a separate, inspectable world model, planner, and goal) so each part can be checked."]}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Dualistic vs embedded agents."}]," The standard picture (an agent with clean I/O, \"larger than\" and \"outside\" its environment, holding a full world model in its head) is dualistic. A real agent is ",["$","em",null,{"children":"embedded"}],": part of the world, made of the same pieces, smaller than its environment, with no crisp boundary, and able to be copied, modified, or to model itself. Embeddedness is what makes self-improvement, multi-agent reasoning about copies, and self-reference unavoidable, and it is what the rest of the day is about."]}],"\n"]}]
2f:["$","h4",null,{"id":"222-fundamental-reading","children":"2.2.2 Fundamental reading"}]

The canonical statement of the embedded-agency problem cluster (the Alexei/Emmy framing the lecture uses)."]}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","a",null,{"href":"https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/FWvzwCDRgcjb9sigb/why-agent-foundations-an-overly-abstract-explanation","children":"Why agent foundations"}],": read entirely. Why this abstract, theory-first approach is worth pursuing."]}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","a",null,{"href":"https://www.lesswrong.com/w/reflectively-consistent-degree-of-freedom","children":"Reflectively consistent degree of freedom"}],": read entirely. The precise notion behind a property an agent would not self-modify away, which is the day's recurring theme of reflective stability."]}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","a",null,{"href":"https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/6mysMAqvo9giHC4iX/what-s-general-purpose-search-and-why-might-we-expect-to-see","children":"General purpose search"}],": read entirely. Why a capable mind plausibly contains a retargetable search process."]}],"\n"]}]
31:["$","h4",null,{"id":"223-the-five-strands-pre-reading-tracks","children":"2.2.3 The five strands (pre-reading tracks)"}]
32:["$","p",null,{"children":["$","em",null,{"children":"The five strands are different facets of one problem, embedded agency; each student pre-reads one. Every summary below is self-contained at the conceptual level and ends with that strand's readings (start with the first listed, which is the most conceptual). The morning lecture previews all five; the cross-pollination discussion connects them."}]}]
33:["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"1. Consequentialist foundations."}]," If an agent's preferences are cyclic, an adversary can money-pump it through trades that each look acceptable but leave it strictly worse off. So any agent that reliably avoids such dominated strategies behaves ",["$","em",null,{"children":"as if"}]," it maximizes a utility function. The ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"complete class theorem"}]," sharpens this: any decision rule that is not dominated (Pareto-optimal across environments) is Bayes-optimal under some prior with full support. This is ",["$","em",null,{"children":"representational"}],", not mechanistic: it says a capable, non-self-defeating agent looks like a Bayesian expected-utility maximizer from the outside, without saying it has a utility function inside, and without telling us ",["$","em",null,{"children":"which"}]," utility function (the hard part). ",["$","em",null,{"children":"Takeaway: coherence gives a reflectively-stable target (a dominated strategy is one a rational agent would self-modify away from), but it under-determines the agent's actual goals."}]," ",["$","em",null,{"children":"Readings:"}]," ",["$","a",null,{"href":"https://www.lesswrong.com/s/SgomvxZ3cJWy2SBCu/p/RQpNHSiWaXTvDxt6R","children":"Coherent decisions imply consistent utilities"}]," (Introduction; \"Why not circular preferences?\"; \"Probabilities and expected utilities\" through \"Conditional probability\"; Conclusion); ",["$","a",null,{"href":"https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/73pTioGZKNcfQmvGF/the-measuring-stick-of-utility-problem","children":"The measuring stick of utility"}],"; ",["$","a",null,{"href":"https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/sZuw6SGfmZHvcAAEP/complete-class-consequentialist-foundations","children":"Complete class: consequentialist foundations"}],"."]}]
34:["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"2. Löb's theorem and tiling agents."}]," A capable agent may build a successor more capable than itself. By ",["$","em",null,{"children":"Vingean reflection"}],", it cannot verify the successor by simulating it (if it could predict the successor's exact moves, it would be that capable already), so it must reason abstractly about the successor's ",["$","em",null,{"children":"design"}],". The natural strategy (\"trust the successor because it only takes actions it has proved safe\") needs the parent to trust the successor's proofs. ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"Löb's theorem"}]," blocks this: if a consistent system ",["$","code",null,{"children":"L"}]," can prove \"if ",["$","code",null,{"children":"L"}]," proves ",["$","code",null,{"children":"C"}]," then ",["$","code",null,{"children":"C"}],"\", then ",["$","code",null,{"children":"L"}]," already proves ",["$","code",null,{"children":"C"}],". A consistent system cannot vouch for its own proofs in the abstract, only for a strictly weaker system's. So a naive chain of self-improvements uses an ever-weaker proof system (a \"telomere\" of logical strength that runs out), the ",["$","em",null,{"children":"finite descent problem"}],". The tiling-agents programme studies whether this obstacle can be overcome. ",["$","em",null,{"children":"Takeaway: self-trust under self-modification is not free; it runs straight into a logical wall."}]," ",["$","em",null,{"children":"Readings:"}]," ",["$","a",null,{"href":"https://intelligence.org/files/lob-notes-IAFF.pdf","children":"Introduction to Löb's theorem"}]," (up to and including section 3); ",["$","a",null,{"href":"https://www.lesswrong.com/w/vingean-reflection","children":"Vingean reflection"}],"; ",["$","a",null,{"href":"https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/QGrX3qK3qxQYK9D4C/walkthrough-of-the-tiling-agents-for-self-modifying-ai-paper","children":"Walkthrough of the tiling agents paper"}]," (start through \"Finite Descent Problem\", then \"What self-modifying agents need\")."]}]
35:["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"3. Logical induction."}]," Standard Bayesian reasoning assumes ",["$","em",null,{"children":"logical omniscience"}],": the agent instantly knows all consequences of its beliefs. A bounded agent cannot (it may know a program's source yet not its output, or the axioms yet not whether a number is prime). ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"Logical induction"}]," (Garrabrant et al.) handles this by picturing a market that prices logical sentences in [0,1]; the ",["$","em",null,{"children":"logical-induction criterion"}]," requires only that no efficient (polynomial-time) trader can exploit the market for unbounded profit, a computable weakening of the Dutch-book argument. That single condition yields convergence and coherence in the limit, timely learning of statistical patterns (it prices \"the nth digit of pi is 7\" near 1/10 without computing it), and ",["$","em",null,{"children":"self-trust"}]," (current credence equals a weighted average of expected future credences). ",["$","em",null,{"children":"Takeaway: a principled model of how a bounded agent should hold probabilities over facts it has not yet computed."}]," ",["$","em",null,{"children":"Readings:"}]," ",["$","a",null,{"href":"https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/y5GftLezdozEHdXkL/an-intuitive-guide-to-garrabrant-induction","children":"An intuitive guide to Garrabrant induction"}],"; ",["$","a",null,{"href":"https://arxiv.org/pdf/1609.03543","children":"Logical induction"}]," (chapters 1 and 3; skim chapter 4)."]}]
62:T80b,<span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut" style="height:0.8193em;vertical-align:-0.136em;"></span><span class="mord">Δ</span><span class="mord mathnormal" style="margin-right:0.0813em;">H</span><span class="mspace" style="margin-right:0.2778em;"></span><span class="mrel">≤</span><span class="mspace" style="margin-right:0.2778em;"></span></span><span class="base"><span class="strut" style="height:0.9664em;vertical-align:-0.2831em;"></span><span class="mord">Δ</span><span class="mord"><span class="mord mathnormal" style="margin-right:0.0813em;">H</span><span class="msupsub"><span class="vlist-t vlist-t2"><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist" style="height:0.6644em;"><span style="top:-2.4169em;margin-left:-0.0813em;margin-right:0.05em;"><span class="pstrut" style="height:2.7em;"></span><span class="sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight"><span class="mord mtight"><span class="mord text mtight"><span class="mord mtight">blind</span></span></span></span></span><span style="top:-3.063em;margin-right:0.05em;"><span class="pstrut" style="height:2.7em;"></span><span class="sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight"><span class="mord mtight"><span class="mop mtight"><span class="mtight">m</span><span class="mtight">a</span><span class="mtight">x</span></span></span></span></span></span><span class="vlist-s">​</span></span><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist" style="height:0.2831em;"><span></span></span></span></span></span></span><span class="mspace" style="margin-right:0.2222em;"></span><span class="mbin">+</span><span class="mspace" style="margin-right:0.2222em;"></span></span><span class="base"><span class="strut" style="height:1em;vertical-align:-0.25em;"></span><span class="mord mathnormal" style="margin-right:0.0785em;">I</span><span class="mopen">(</span><span class="mord mathnormal" style="margin-right:0.0785em;">X</span><span class="mpunct">;</span><span class="mspace" style="margin-right:0.1667em;"></span><span class="mord mathnormal">A</span><span class="mclose">)</span></span></span>36:["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"4. Optimization and thermodynamics."}]," A powerful agent reliably steers the world into a narrow region of outcomes, ones extremely unlikely under any random process. This is ",["$","em",null,{"children":"local entropy reduction"}],": concentrating probability mass from a broad initial distribution onto a narrow target. Even a pure predictor has an objective reason to attend to optimizers: naming what an optimizer steers toward predicts the outcome cheaply and robustly, where modelling the initial conditions would be expensive and chaos-fragile. Steering is bounded by information: the ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"Touchette-Lloyd"}]," inequality says the entropy reduction a sighted agent achieves over a blind baseline is at most the mutual information between its observations and actions (",["$","span",null,{"className":"katex","role":"math","aria-label":"\\Delta H \\leq \\Delta H_{\\text{blind}}^{\\max}+ I(X;A)","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"$62"}}],"). ",["$","em",null,{"children":"Algorithmic thermodynamics"}]," (Ebtekar and Hutter) replaces ensemble entropy with Kolmogorov complexity, giving laws for individual states and making an embedded agent's knowledge an endogenous physical quantity (algorithmic mutual information between memory and environment), which is exactly its budget for optimization. ","$L63"," ","$L64"," the self-contained note ","$L65"," (read entirely; appendix optional) is the primary reading; its underlying sources are ","$L66"," (up to and including \"Relationship to Garrabrant and Demski's Embedded Agency\"), ","$L67",", and ","$L68","."]}]
37:["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"5. Descriptive agent foundations."}]," ",["$","em",null,{"children":"Normative"}]," agent foundations asks what an ideal agent should look like; ",["$","em",null,{"children":"descriptive"}]," asks what agents actually arising in the world (bacteria, neural networks, future AI) look like, and aims to read off their goals, world model, and decision structure from the outside. It works bottom-up from properties of the world (modularity, selection pressures, computational limits). ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"Selection theorems"}]," aim to prove results of the form \"any system selected to achieve goal G in environment E must contain structure approximately isomorphic to X\", giving mechanistic rather than merely representational accounts of agency. A key example: the world's ",["$","em",null,{"children":"modularity"}]," (it decomposes into sparsely-interacting subsystems) is what makes both world-modelling (Bayesian networks propagate updates locally) and planning (general-purpose search can pursue decoupled subgoals) tractable. ",["$","em",null,{"children":"Takeaway: the complementary direction to coherence: not \"non-dominated agents can be described as maximizers\" but \"what pressures make agent-like structure actually arise.\""}]," ",["$","em",null,{"children":"Readings:"}]," ",["$","a",null,{"href":"https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/G2Lne2Fi7Qra5Lbuf/selection-theorems-a-program-for-understanding-agents","children":"Selection theorems: a program for understanding agents"}],"; ",["$","a",null,{"href":"https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/TiBsZ9beNqDHEvXt4/how-we-picture-bayesian-agents","children":"How we picture Bayesian agents"}],"; ",["$","a",null,{"href":"https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/RuDD3aQWLDSb4eTXP/what-selection-theorems-do-we-expect-want","children":"What selection theorems do we expect/want"}],"."]}]

The specific discussion prompts used are listed in the teaching guide."}]

3d:["$","ul",null,{"children":["\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Exercise 1: Gödel's second incompleteness theorem"}]," (difficulty 4/5, importance 4/5). 8f:["$","p",null,{"children":["One might hope to work around this by having the parent use a ",["$","em",null,{"children":"stronger"}]," proof system than the child: a stronger system ",["$","em",null,{"children":"can"}]," trust a weaker one's proofs.
